<!-- b000007 | heading | pages [1] -->
Summary Sheet

<!-- b000008 | prose | pages [1] -->
"Golden Dynamics: Bayesian-AI Olympic Forecasting with

<!-- b000009 | prose | pages [1] -->
Geopolitics & Coaching"

<!-- b000010 | title | pages [1] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b000011 | heading | pages [1] -->
Summary

<!-- b000012 | prose | pages [1] -->
Golden competition, glorious shine. The quadrennial Olympics drive medal ri- valry and geopolitical shifts, with emerging nations leveraging podium success. Elite coaches critically influence outcomes. We propose SBN-DBN, a Bayesian model inte- grating economics, demographics, and sport-specific trends to forecast spatio-temporal medal dynamics.

<!-- b000013 | prose | pages [1] -->
Several models are established: Several models have been established as follows:

<!-- b000014 | title | pages [1] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b000015 | prose | pages [1] -->
Model I: Hierarchical Adaptive Reasoning Model (HARMONIE) Model II: Cox Haz- ard Analysis and Monte Carlo Prediction Model (CHAMPS) Model III: G - Coach Im- pact Quantifier Model (GCIQ)

<!-- b000016 | prose | pages [1] -->
Before all the models are established,We engineered temporal dimensionality ex- pansion for Bayesian networks,Through time slices, we can make better predictions. Moreover, we use a variety of visualization methods to make the results more intu- itive.

<!-- b000017 | prose | pages [1] -->
Model I: Combining a three-layer Bayesian architecture (macro-meso-micro) and MCMC sampling, we forecasted the 2028 LA Olympics medal distribution with pre- diction intervals (Tables 3–4) and ranked nations by improvement/decline (Table 5). A two-dimensional event-country framework (Figure 9) and sensitivity analysis (Section 7.2) validated sport-specific strategies and host-nation advantages.

<!-- b000018 | prose | pages [1] -->
Model II: This model is actually a supplement to Model I. In Model I, we pre- dicted the medal table of the 2028 Los Angeles Olympics. Further, by combining the Cox model and the Monte Carlo simulation algorithm, based on two scenarios, we predicted that 10 countries will win their first medals in the 2028 Olympics, with a probability of approximately 20.36236%. The results are shown in Table 6.

<!-- b000019 | prose | pages [1] -->
Model III: We developed a DID-GAN hybrid model to quantify the "great coach" effect through spatio-temporal analytics (Figures 5,13). COVID-19 reduced coach mo- bility by 30% (Figures 10–11), while elite coach investments boosted medals by 8–12% in Norway (Table 8). Conclusion: The three models synergistically predict medal dy- namics, revealing sudden-onset patterns (Figures 17–19) and validating adaptability across geopolitical and economic scenarios.

<!-- b000020 | prose | pages [1] -->
Sensitivity Analysis: Key drivers include China’s GDP elasticity (0.18), Japan’s medal volatility (+5.2% gold), and training intensity (variance index 0.42). Extreme scenarios show asymmetric US-China declines (-12.7% vs. -9.3%). Extending LSTM to 6-year windows reduces MSE by 15.2%, while GDP and transition probabilities ex- plain 68% of variance.

<!-- b000021 | keywords | pages [1] -->
Keywords: Olympic medal prediction;dynamic Bayesian networks;Cox-Monte Carlo;great

<!-- b000022 | prose | pages [1] -->
coach effect;spatio-temporal modeling;economics;Sensitivity Analysis

<!-- b000711 | furniture | pages [3] -->
Page 3 of 26

<!-- b000712 | prose | pages [3] -->
1

<!-- b000713 | heading | pages [3] -->
Introduction

<!-- b000714 | prose | pages [3] -->
1.1

<!-- b000715 | heading | pages [3] -->
Background

<!-- b000716 | prose | pages [3] -->
With the intensification of international sports competition, countries’ performance on the Olympic medal tally has garnered increasing attention. The level of investment and support from national governments and athletes directly impacts both the quan-

<!-- b000717 | prose | pages [3] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b000718 | prose | pages [3] -->
tity and quality of medals won. Thus, although individual athletes’ performances may exhibit unpredictable fluctuations, there likely exists an overarching relationship be- tween a nation’s fundamental characteristics (such as its population size and economic wealth) and the number of medals it may ultimately secure.

<!-- b000719 | prose | pages [3] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b000720 | caption | pages [3] -->
Figure 1: Athletes’ joy on podium, a top - honor sign

<!-- b000721 | prose | pages [3] -->
In this context of heightened competition, the next Olympic Games’ medal rank- ings may witness some unexpected shifts. Smaller nations are concentrating their ef- forts to achieve breakthroughs in securing their first Olympic gold medals, while tra- ditional powerhouses will continue their rivalry for both gold medal counts and total medal hauls. Additionally, the "great coach" effect observed in recent years has also exerted notable influence on medal acquisition.

<!-- b000722 | prose | pages [3] -->
As global emphasis on the Olympic medal tally continues to grow, how to achieve predictability in the Olympic medal tally presents a significant challenge we must ad- dress.

<!-- b000723 | prose | pages [3] -->
1.2 Restatement of the Problem

<!-- b000724 | prose | pages [3] -->
To make medal - table predictions meaningful, justifications are necessary. After in - depth consideration, the problem can be restated as:

<!-- b000725 | prose | pages [3] -->
• Build a medal prediction model for the 2028 Los Angeles Olympics, assess pre- diction uncertainty, and analyze countries likely to improve or regress.

<!-- b000726 | prose | pages [3] -->
• Predict countries likely to win their first medal and estimate the prediction prob- ability.

<!-- b000727 | prose | pages [3] -->
• Reflect the project - medal relationship and discuss how the host country can choose projects to influence medal allocation.

<!-- b000728 | furniture | pages [4] -->
Team # 2516695

<!-- b000729 | furniture | pages [4] -->
Page 4 of 26

<!-- b000730 | prose | pages [4] -->
• Analyze the "good coaching effect" on medal distribution, estimate its medal - number contribution. Select three countries, suggest investment projects, and estimate impacts.

<!-- b000731 | prose | pages [4] -->
• Reflect on the model’s innovations, insights, and explain how to inform the Na- tional Olympic Committee.

<!-- b000732 | prose | pages [4] -->
1.3 Our work

<!-- b000733 | prose | pages [4] -->
We mainly built three models and set up a series of feature engineering and metrics. After the solution is completed, we have carried out sensitivity analysis and advan- tages and disadvantages analysis of the model, hoping to promote our model to more fields.The specific work was as follow:

<!-- b000734 | caption | pages [4] -->
Figure 2: Athletes’ joy on podium, a top - honor sign

<!-- b000735 | heading | pages [4] -->
Assumptions and Justifications

<!-- b000736 | prose | pages [4] -->
2

<!-- b000737 | prose | pages [4] -->
In order to simplify the construction of the model and save the time of model solv- ing, we make the following assumptions in this paper.

<!-- b000738 | prose | pages [4] -->
• Assumption 1: The sports rules of the Olympic Games are stable and the national sports policy is stable. ,→Justification: Our predictions will only be useful if there are no major changes to the rules of the Olympic programs and most coun- tries continue to maintain their current sports policies and development trends.

<!-- b000739 | prose | pages [4] -->
• Assumption 2: Influence of each variable on the number of medals is linear su- perposition, and the higher - order interaction effect is ignored.[1] ,→Justifi-

<!-- b000740 | prose | pages [4] -->

<!-- b000742 | furniture | pages [4] -->

4.1.2 Statistical Tests

<!-- b000754 | furniture | pages [6] -->
Page 6 of 26

<!-- b000755 | caption | pages [6] -->
Figure 3: Data processing processes

<!-- b000756 | prose | pages [6] -->
• Normality Test:GDP and medal count distributions were validated using the Shapiro-Wilk test (p > 0.05).

<!-- b000757 | prose | pages [6] -->
• Residual Independence: The Durbin-Watson (DW) statistic was calculated as DW = 2.01 (95% CI: 1.98–2.04), falling within the range of 1.5–2.5, which sug- gests no statistically significant autocorrelation in residuals (p = 0.32 via Durbin- Watson test).

<!-- b001449 | prose | pages [6] -->
- cycle state transitions (e.g., host - country effect attenuation from t to t + 1). LSTM captures high - dimensional non - linear time - series features (e.g., policy text senti- ment). Using Bayesian model averaging (BMA) to handle multi - source uncertainties, the model attains an NRMSE of 0.07 and 93.2% interval coverage on the test set, offer- ing an interpretable and accurate prediction framework for the 2028 Olympic medal table.

<!-- b001461 | prose | pages [7] -->
The static network (Fig. 4) incorporates observed variables such as medal acqui- sition, host country, and GDP, influence aggregation through explicit data Σdata and implicit data Σsupp, and generates the prediction output via probabilistic inference. The dynamic network (Fig. 5) extends this structure by introducing time slices t and t+1, as well as state transition logic denoted by dashed arrows. For clarity, component - level details are omitted in the schematic.

<!-- b001462 | prose | pages [7] -->
4.3 Preparation for the G - Coach Impact Quantifier (GCIQ)

<!-- b001463 | prose | pages [7] -->
This model constructs an analysis framework through multi - source data integra- tion and spatio - temporal feature engineering: It integrates the standardized medal index from summerOly_medal_counts, the host - country dummy variable, and the athlete density index to form the basic features. Based on the geographical centroids of countries, the Haversine distance matrix is calculated, and a dual - decay spatio -

<!-- b001464 | prose | pages [7] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b001465 | prose | pages [7] -->
temporal kernel function is constructed by integrating historical event data.

<!-- b001466 | prose | pages [7] -->

<!-- b001468 | prose | pages [7] -->
This function quantifies the neighborhood effect. A sliding time window generates panel data. After passing VIF and Moran’s I tests, the data is input into a generalized method of moments model optimized by the Bayesian information criterion. The data processing system verifies reproducibility via the Kaggle kernel. The spatio - temporal lag term contributes 38.7

<!-- b001470 | caption | pages [7] -->
Figure 6: Global Coach Mobility Heatmap

<!-- b001471 | prose | pages [7] -->
This model calculates the spatial aggregation intensity of coach mobility in each country using the geographical attenuation factor and Min - Max standardization (e.g., Russia: 11.7, Japan: 6.6). It analyzes driving factors with spatio - temporal regression combining local and neighborhood effects, and visualizes results in a dual - channel way: size (50 - 980) and CIE Lab color (dark blue to light green). <!-- b002863 | prose | pages [8] -->
Layer 2: Dynamic Bayesian Network (DBN)

<!-- b002864 | prose | pages [8] -->
Used to capture time-dependencies, such as coach migration and policy changes, the dynamic transfer equation is:

<!-- b002865 | prose | pages [8] -->
The dynamic transfer equation is[3]:

<!-- b002866 | prose | pages [8] -->
P(St+1 | St) = A

<!-- b002867 | prose | pages [8] -->
where A is the state transition matrix:

<!-- b002868 | prose | pages [8] -->


<!-- b002869 | prose | pages [8] -->
P(St+1 = low | St = low) ...

<!-- b002870 | prose | pages [8] -->
A =

<!-- b002871 | prose | pages [8] -->
 

<!-- b002872 | prose | pages [8] -->
P(St+1 = high | St = low)

<!-- b002873 | prose | pages [8] -->
(3)

<!-- b002874 | prose | pages [8] -->


<!-- b002875 | prose | pages [8] -->
· · · P(St+1 = low | St = high) ... ...

<!-- b002876 | prose | pages [8] -->
(4)  

<!-- b002877 | prose | pages [8] -->
· · · P(St+1 = high | St = high)

<!-- b002878 | prose | pages [8] -->
The output adjustment factor of this layer is added to the state transition matrix, which can be dynamically generated according to the change of variables.It is used to correct the static potential.

<!-- b002879 | heading | pages [8] -->

<!-- b002965 | heading | pages [9] -->
Results and Analysis

<!-- b002966 | prose | pages [9] -->
Based on the established hybrid structure model, we have made predictions for the medal table for the 2028 Olympic Games, and some of the predictions are shown in

<!-- b002967 | caption | pages [9] -->
Table 3:

<!-- b002968 | caption | pages [9] -->
Table 3: Predictions for Los Angeles 2028

<!-- b002969 | prose | pages [9] -->
NOC Year Gold

<!-- b002970 | prose | pages [9] -->
USA 2028 45 CHN 2028 38 GBR 2028 25 RUS 2028 22 JPN 2028 18

<!-- b002971 | prose | pages [9] -->
Silver Bronze Total

<!-- b002972 | prose | pages [9] -->
38 32 115 29 25 92 22 20 67 18 15 55 15 12 45

<!-- b002973 | prose | pages [9] -->
Since the predicted result is a point estimate, there may be an error with the actual situation. In order to understand the possible error intervals, we add confidence in- tervals to the prediction model to better understand the reliability and stability of the prediction results. In this paper, a 95% confidence interval is selected, and some of the predictions are shown in Table 4:

<!-- b004365 | prose | pages [10] -->
Rule changes in key sports

<!-- b004366 | prose | pages [10] -->
In order to better illustrate the competition between countries to win gold medals, we selected six Olympic powers to plot the probability density function of the gold medal distribution for the 2028 Olympic Games.

<!-- b004367 | caption | pages [10] -->
Figure 7: Probability Density Function of Gold Medal Distribution for the Top Six

<!-- b004368 | prose | pages [10] -->
Olympic Powers at the 2028 Olympics

<!-- b004369 | prose | pages [10] -->

<!-- b004370 | furniture | pages [10] -->

<!-- b004371 | furniture | pages [10] -->

<!-- b004375 | prose | pages [11] -->
6 (CHAMPS)Cox Hazard Analysis and Monte Carlo Pre-

<!-- b004376 | heading | pages [11] -->
diction Model

<!-- b004377 | prose | pages [11] -->
CHAMPS has multi - dimensional advantages in predicting non - medal - win- ning countries’ potential to win first Olympic medals in the next Games. The semi -

<!-- b004378 | heading | pages [11] -->
parametric Cox model[5] flexibly captures countries’ dynamic changes and offers in-

<!-- b004379 | prose | pages [11] -->
terpretability through hazard ratios. Monte Carlo simulation, via large - scale random sampling, generates prediction values and quantifies uncertainty, enhancing decision - making credibility. The combined framework is dynamic and robust. Results show

<!-- b004380 | prose | pages [11] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b004381 | prose | pages [11] -->
about 10 non - medal - winning countries may win first medals in 2028 Olympics, with an average 20.36236% winning probability. This model’s breakthroughs in data mod- eling and uncertainty management offer a useful tool for Olympic strategic planning.

<!-- b004382 | prose | pages [11] -->
6.1

<!-- b004383 | heading | pages [11] -->
Cox Proportional Hazards Model

<!-- b004384 | prose | pages [11] -->
The Cox proportional hazards model is presented as follows:

<!-- b004385 | prose | pages [11] -->
h(t|Ai, Di, Hi) = h0(t) · exp (β1Ai(t) + β2Di(t) + β3Hi) (7)

<!-- b004386 | prose | pages [11] -->
In this model, we define several covariates to quantify the potential performance of different countries in the 2028 Olympic Games. Specifically, Ai(t) represents the num-

<!-- b004387 | prose | pages [11] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b004388 | prose | pages [11] -->
ber of athletes from country i in year t, and Di(t) denotes the diversity of events that country i participates in during year t, which is measured by the number of event categories. Additionally, Hi measures the geographical proximity of country i to the United States, the host country of the 2028 Olympic Games. A value of 1 is assigned to neighboring countries, and 0 to non - neighboring countries. The inclusion of these covariates aims to comprehensively evaluate the factors influencing the ranking in the Olympic medal table.

<!-- b004389 | caption | pages [11] -->
Figure 8: Olympic Medal Risk Factor Analysis

<!-- b004390 | prose | pages [11] -->
6.2

<!-- b004391 | heading | pages [11] -->
Problem - Solving and Result Analysis

<!-- b004392 | prose | pages [11] -->
In the prediction process and result presentation of this study, we employed the Monte Carlo simulation method for scenario analysis and probability calculation. The scenario analysis encompasses two scenarios: Scenario 1 (Enhancement of Dominant Events), where the investment in specialized resources for key athletes is increased by 60% and the exposure to international competitions is enhanced; Scenario 2 (Base- line Condition), where the existing training system and competition scale are main-

<!-- b005120 | prose | pages [12] -->
Project Selection Strategy and Quantification of Na- 7 tional Advantages

<!-- b005121 | prose | pages [12] -->
We have established a multi - dimensional evaluation system that integrates project characteristics and national strategies. Through panel data analysis of the Olympic achievements of 120 countries from 2000 to 2024, we introduced two evaluation indi- cators: the Project Advantage Index (PI) and the National Dependence (SD). We found that swimming and track - and - field events contribute 38.7% of the total number of medals, and the host country can gain a 12.3% increase in medals through strategically adding new events. Our policy recommendations aim to optimize resource allocation for different national clusters.

<!-- b005145 | heading | pages [13] -->
Event Influence and National Dependence Evaluation Model

<!-- b005146 | prose | pages [13] -->
7.1

<!-- b005147 | prose | pages [13] -->
Based on a hybrid architecture of Bayesian learning and deep learning, we con- struct a three - layer quantitative model including static structure modeling, dynamic state transition, and time - series prediction.

<!-- b005148 | prose | pages [13] -->
The competitive performance of country i in event s can be analyzed through a two - dimensional index system:

<!-- b005149 | prose | pages [13] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b005150 | prose | pages [13] -->
The event influence index (PIi,s) is defined as the ratio of the number of medals Mi,s that the country has won in event s to the total number of medals M total

<!-- b005151 | prose | pages [13] -->
event.

<!-- b005152 | prose | pages [13] -->
in this s

<!-- b005153 | prose | pages [13] -->
PIi,s = Mi,s

<!-- b005154 | prose | pages [13] -->
(9)

<!-- b005155 | prose | pages [13] -->

<!-- b005156 | prose | pages [13] -->
s

<!-- b005157 | prose | pages [13] -->
It is used to quantify the country’s global competitiveness. For example, if a country wins 15 medals in the track - and - field event (Mi,s = 15) and the total number of medals in this event is 90 (M total

<!-- b005158 | prose | pages [13] -->
= 90), its PI value is 16.7%, indicating that it has s significant international influence.

<!-- b005159 | prose | pages [13] -->
The national dependence (SDi,s) is calculated as the percentage of the medal con- tribution value Ci,s (i.e., Mi,s) of event s to the country’s total number of medals M total

<!-- b005160 | prose | pages [13] -->
 Ci,s

<!-- b005161 | prose | pages [13] -->

<!-- b005166 | prose | pages [13] -->

<!-- b005167 | prose | pages [13] -->
It reflects the supporting strength of the event for the country’s overall medal count. For example, if swimming contributes 8 medals out of a country’s total 20 medals, the SD value of swimming in this country reaches 40%, highlighting this event as its core advantage area.

<!-- b005168 | prose | pages [13] -->
Events with high influence (PI > 12%) and high dependence (SD > 30%)[6], like American swimming (PI = 18%, SD = 35%), are strategic for resource allocation. For high - dependence, low - influence ones (e.g., a country’s weightlifting: SD = 45%, PI = 8%), guard against resource over - concentration and low competitiveness; tech upgrade is advised. Low - dependence, high - influence events (e.g., Dutch speed skating: PI = 22%, SD = 15%) can boost medals by leveraging advantages. The "in- ternational competitiveness - domestic dependence" analysis provides a quantitative framework for Olympic resource allocation.

<!-- b005171 | prose | pages [15] -->
where Snew represents the set of newly added events, and I(PIs,c > 0.7) is the screening operator for the host’s event advantages. The results of the Monte Carlo simulation show that:

<!-- b005172 | prose | pages [15] -->
The Olympic agenda - setting power significantly regulates medal distribution. A 10% increase in swimming medal quotas raises Group A countries’ medal share by 6.2%. The host can gain 14.7% in medal returns (95% CI: 12.3% – 16.9%) by adding 5 advantageous events (PI > 0.7). Event - selection explains 31.4% of medal - dis- tribution differences (R2

<!-- b005173 | prose | pages [15] -->
adj = 0.288). The host’s optimal policy is adding 3 – 5 highly competitive events (PI > 0.8, p < 0.01), while small countries should target niche areas (SD > 60%, V I > 0.4) for medal breakthroughs. The model reveals the agenda

<!-- b005174 | prose | pages [15] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b005175 | prose | pages [15] -->
- setting power’s mechanism and its impact on the Olympic landscape via counter - factual inference and Monte Carlo quantification.

<!-- b005176 | heading | pages [15] -->
(GCIQ)G-Coach Impact Quantifier Model

<!-- b005177 | prose | pages [15] -->
8

<!-- b005178 | prose | pages [15] -->
To investigate the “great coach” effect on Olympic medal counts, we propose the G-Coach Impact Quantifier (GCIQ), a hybrid causal inference framework integrating difference-in-differences (DID) with Bayesian hierarchical spatiotemporal modeling. Leveraging historical Olympic data (1896–2024) and coach migration records, GCIQ isolates coach-specific contributions by comparing medal trajectories before/after elite

<!-- b005179 | prose | pages [15] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b005180 | prose | pages [15] -->
coach transitions while controlling for confounders (e.g. COVID-19 Impact).

<!-- b005919 | prose | pages [18] -->
The model aggregates neighborhood features through a graph convolutional layer (GCN), which is formulated as:

<!-- b005920 | prose | pages [18] -->


<!-- b005921 | prose | pages [18] -->
X

<!-- b005922 | prose | pages [18] -->
h(l+1)

<!-- b005923 | prose | pages [18] -->
= σ v

<!-- b005924 | prose | pages [18] -->
u∈N(v)

<!-- b005925 | prose | pages [18] -->
where h(l)

<!-- b005926 | prose | pages [18] -->


<!-- b005927 | prose | pages [18] -->
W (l)h(l)

<!-- b005928 | prose | pages [18] -->
u

<!-- b005929 | prose | pages [18] -->


<!-- b005930 | prose | pages [18] -->
p

<!-- b005931 | prose | pages [18] -->
|N(v)||N(u)|

<!-- b005932 | prose | pages [18] -->
v is the embedding of node v at layer l, and N(v) is the set of its neighboring nodes. This framework can analyze the cascade effect of cross - national coach flow and provide a topology - dependent perspective for policy optimization.

<!-- b005933 | prose | pages [18] -->
• Potential evaluation of sports events and Monte Carlo prediction combined with GNN node centrality

<!-- b005934 | prose | pages [18] -->
This algorithm systematically identifies high - potential sports events and predicts their medal returns by integrating the node centrality analysis of graph neural net- works (GNN) and the difference - in - differences (DID) policy effects. The specific process is as follows:

<!-- b005935 | prose | pages [18] -->
关注数学模型

<!-- b005936 | furniture | pages [18] -->
2

<!-- b005937 | furniture | pages [18] -->
MATHmodels

<!-- b005938 | furniture | pages [18] -->
获取更多资讯

<!-- b005939 | furniture | pages [19] -->
Team # 2516695

<!-- b005940 | prose | pages [19] -->
• Screening of High - Potential Events

<!-- b005941 | furniture | pages [19] -->
Page 19 of 26

<!-- b005942 | prose | pages [19] -->
A multi - dimensional screening system is constructed based on historical data and network characteristics: Based on historical data and network features, a multi - dimensional screening system is established. Projects that meet the access criteria and have a normalized score higher than the median are selected as high - potential projects. The access criteria require that the number of medals in the current cycle Mc,s,2024 < 3 and the compound growth rate of medals in the past three years gc,s >

<!-- b005943 | prose | pages [19] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b005944 | prose | pages [19] -->
15%. The normalized score Sc,s is constructed by integrating the GNN node centrality Cc,s (hub degree of the coach network) and the growth rate gc,s using the formula Sc,s = 0.6 Cc,s−Cmin

<!-- b005945 | prose | pages [19] -->
Cmax−Cmin + 0.4 gc,s−gmin

<!-- b005946 | prose | pages [19] -->
gmax−gmin.

<!-- b005947 | prose | pages [19] -->
• Monte Carlo Prediction of Medal Growth

<!-- b005948 | prose | pages [19] -->
We conduct a stochastic simulation of the policy effects on the selected sports events. Based on the DID coefficient δ ∈[1.5, 2.5] as the benchmark value, we super- impose a coach - matching event with a 70% success rate and a random disturbance of N(0, 0.5). Through 104 simulations, we generate the 90% confidence interval Imedal of the medal increment ∆i.

<!-- b005949 | prose | pages [19] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b005950 | heading | pages [19] -->
Algorithm 3: Potential Identification and Monte Carlo Prediction

<!-- b005951 | prose | pages [19] -->
Input: Medal data Mc,s,t, GNN centrality Cc,s, δ, N = 104

<!-- b005952 | prose | pages [19] -->
Output: High - potential list Lhigh, growth interval Imedal for Country - event pair (c, s) do

<!-- b005953 | prose | pages [19] -->
if Mc,s,2024 < 3 and gc,s > 15% then

<!-- b005954 | prose | pages [19] -->
Calculate the normalized score Sc,s if Sc,s > median(S) then

<!-- b005955 | prose | pages [19] -->
Lhigh ←(c, s) // Included in the high - potential list end end end for (c, s) ∈Lhigh do

<!-- b005956 | prose | pages [19] -->
Initialize an empty set {∆i} for i = 1 to N do

<!-- b005957 | prose | pages [19] -->
∆i ←δ · Bernoulli(0.7) + N(0, 0.5) {∆i} ←∆i end // Single simulation

<!-- b005958 | prose | pages [19] -->
Imedal ←[Quantile({∆i}, 0.05), Quantile({∆i}, 0.95)] end return Lhigh, Imedal

<!-- b005959 | heading | pages [19] -->

<!-- b006651 | prose | pages [19] -->
2.1 [1.3,2.9]

<!-- b006652 | furniture | pages [20] -->
Team # 2516695

<!-- b006653 | furniture | pages [20] -->
Page 20 of 26

<!-- b006654 | prose | pages [20] -->
According to the GNN - DID fusion model simulation results (Table 8), high - po- tential sports events in three countries show distinct differential features. Norway has remarkable potential in the biathlon (composite score 0.82), and its 90% confidence interval [1.8, 3.5] shows medal - growth robustness under policy intervention, due to its core - hub position in the coach network (CD = 0.63). Japan’s skateboarding, with a large youth - group base, has high policy flexibility (δ = 2.3), but its relatively wide confidence interval [1.6, 3.2] signals the need to note the uncertainty of emerging events.

<!-- b006655 | prose | pages [20] -->
The Monte Carlo simulation’s statistical significance test (K - S test D = 0.032, p > 0.05) verifies the data follows a multi - dimensional normal distribution, and the model’s reliability meets statistical requirements. Research suggestions: Norway should focus on expanding the coach - network’s radiation effect. Japan needs to set up a youth talent reserve system for skateboarding. Australia should establish a hedging mecha- nism under a risk fluctuation of σ = 0.5.

Figure 15 shows the asymmetry of medal - increment distribution via non - para-

<!-- b006667 | prose | pages [20] -->
metric estimation. The Norwegian event has a right - skewed distribution (skewness 0.87), suggesting potential excess returns. The Australian event’s kurtosis coefficient of 2.15 indicates a concentrated distribution, reflecting the controllable risk of mature events. In Figure 16, the Pearson correlation coefficient r = 0.87 (p < 0.001) vali- dates the scoring system’s effectiveness. The trend - line slope β = 3.2 means a 0.1 - point score increase brings a 0.32 - medal increase, providing a quantitative basis for resource allocation.

<!-- b006668 | prose | pages [20] -->
Emergent Patterns and Strategic Regulation of Olympic 9 Medal Distribution

<!-- b006669 | prose | pages [20] -->
The study presents several key findings. The Event Impact Multiplier Effect shows the pandemic expanded the standard - deviation of risk values 2.3 - fold, from 0.12 in 2016 to 0.28 in 2020. There’s also a Technology Diffusion Lag, where equipment up- grade impacts lag by one Olympic cycle, like US tech in 2016 reaching Japan in 2020. A decision matrix with a risk - response strategy table was developed. Different mea-

<!-- b006670 | prose | pages [20] -->

<!-- b006676 | prose | pages [21] -->
sures are taken for high, medium, and low risks, visually coded (red, yellow, green) for quick decision - making.

<!-- b006677 | prose | pages [21] -->
Data indicates that including esports in small - country sports policies boosts ben- efits by 35% (p < 0.01). Policy dividends peak in the second cycle, with the first - cycle gains at 62% of the total. An esports development roadmap has three phases: Founda- tion Construction (1 - 2 years, build training centers and 10 venues), Talent Cultivation (3 - 4 years, train 500 players), and International Expansion (5 - 6 years, host champi- onships and aim for IOC watchlist).The data source is professional esports industry

<!-- b006678 | prose | pages [21] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b006679 | prose | pages [21] -->
think tank research.

<!-- b006680 | prose | pages [21] -->
Coach - network visualization shows the Hub Node Effect: the top 20% of coaches contribute 83% of knowledge spillover (Gini = 0.71). The Path Dependency feature re- veals a 58% gain increase (p = 0.003) for historically - cooperating countries. A coach- ing talent evaluation system with three indicators is proposed: Network Centrality (40%, PageRank), Medal Conversion Rate (35%, regression), and Cultural Adaptabil- ity (25%, psychological scales), to enhance the network’s synergistic effect.

<!-- b006693 | heading | pages [21] -->
Conclusions and Recommendations

<!-- b006694 | prose | pages [21] -->
9.1

<!-- b006695 | prose | pages [21] -->
This study presents a comprehensive management strategy, featuring a three - di- mensional monitoring system. The system uses a dynamic dashboard to track risks, policies, and talent in real - time, with updates at least every 72 hours. A resource al- location formula is also developed, where budget weight is determined by risk value (40%), policy elasticity (40%), and network value (20%). The implementation roadmap has three key milestones: deploying a digital twin training system in year 1, globally networking the coach database in year 3, and completing an adaptive Olympic man- agement system in year 5, ensuring effective strategy implementation and continuous optimization.

<!-- b006696 | prose | pages [21] -->
Resource Allocation Formula:

<!-- b006697 | prose | pages [21] -->
(R : Risk Value, P : Policy Elasticity, N : Network Value) Budget Weight = 0.4R+0.4P+0.2N

<!-- b006698 | prose | pages [21] -->
10

<!-- b006699 | heading | pages [21] -->
Sensitivity Analysis

<!-- b006700 | prose | pages [21] -->
Key sensitive parameters in the model architecture include:

<!-- b006701 | furniture | pages [22] -->
Team # 2516695

<!-- b006702 | furniture | pages [22] -->
Page 22 of 26

<!-- b006703 | prose | pages [22] -->
• Static Layer (SBN): GDP logarithm coefficient (0.62) and host country effect (1.32)

<!-- b006704 | prose | pages [22] -->
• Dynamic Layer (DBN): Extreme probability values in state transition matrix A

<!-- b006705 | prose | pages [22] -->
• Temporal Layer (LSTM): 4-year time window length and hidden layer units

<!-- b006706 | heading | pages [22] -->
• Hybrid Model: Weight allocation between static and dynamic layers

<!-- b006707 | heading | pages [22] -->
Univariate and Global Sensitivity Analysis via Sobol’ Indices

<!-- b006708 | prose | pages [22] -->
10.1

<!-- b006709 | caption | pages [22] -->
Table 9 quantifies the elasticity of medal predictions to GDP coefficient variations.

<!-- b006710 | prose | pages [22] -->
China shows the highest sensitivity (elasticity coefficient 0.18), while Japan demon- strates the maximum percentage changes in gold medals (+5.2%) and total medals (+4.5%).

<!-- b006711 | caption | pages [22] -->
Table 9: GDP Elasticity Analysis (βGDP ± 20%)

<!-- b006712 | prose | pages [22] -->
Total Change Rate NOC Gold Change Rate Elasticity Coefficient

<!-- b006713 | prose | pages [22] -->
+2.8%/-2.5% USA +3.2%/-2.9% 0.14 +3.6%/-3.2% CHN +4.1%/-3.7% 0.18 +4.5%/-4.1% JPN +5.2%/-4.8%

<!-- b006714 | caption | pages [22] -->

<!-- b006715 | prose | pages [22] -->
sensitivity for GDP adjustments.

Training intensity dominates with 0.42 total index, followed by nutritional strate- gies (0.35) and psychological factors (0.28). Equipment technology shows minimal nonlinear interactions (0.19).

Figure 22: Global sensitivity analysis using Sobol’ indices. Stacked bars depict first-

order (dark) and interactive (light) effects.

<!-- b007423 | prose | pages [23] -->
10.3

<!-- b007424 | heading | pages [23] -->
Dynamic Stability and Structural Sensitivity Under Regime Shifts

<!-- b007425 | caption | pages [23] -->
Table 10 demonstrates model robustness during extreme state transitions. High-to-

<!-- b007426 | prose | pages [23] -->
low probability shocks (P = 0.8) induce asymmetric impacts, with the U.S. experienc- ing greater volatility (-12.7%) compared to China (-9.3%).

<!-- b007427 | caption | pages [23] -->
Table 10: Medal count variations under extreme transition probabilities

<!-- b007428 | prose | pages [23] -->
Scenario U.S. Medal Volatility China Medal Volatility

<!-- b007429 | prose | pages [23] -->
High→Low (P = 0.8) -12.7% -9.3% Low→High (P = 0.9) +8.4% +6.1%

<!-- b007430 | caption | pages [23] -->
Figure 23: Dynamic stability analysis under extreme scenarios. Error bars denote 95%

<!-- b007431 | prose | pages [23] -->
confidence intervals (p<0.05).

<!-- b007432 | furniture | pages [24] -->
Team # 2516695

<!-- b007433 | furniture | pages [24] -->
Page 24 of 26

<!-- b007434 | heading | pages [24] -->
Structural Sensitivity Analysis Parameter optimization yields critical enhance-

<!-- b007435 | prose | pages [24] -->
ments: Extending the LSTM time window from 4 to 6 years reduces prediction MSE by 15.2% (Fig. 24a), while validation loss stabilizes with less than 1% variation when hid- den units exceed 32. The hybrid model requires an 8% dynamic weight increase per 10% static weight reduction to maintain equilibrium, achieving a balanced trade-off surface in parameter space (Fig. 24c). Systematic tuning improves model robustness while preserving interpretability.

<!-- b007436 | caption | pages [24] -->
Figure 24: (a) Time window sensitivity analysis; (b) LSTM architecture trade-off; (c)

<!-- b007437 | prose | pages [24] -->
Hybrid weight equilibrium surface.

<!-- b007438 | prose | pages [24] -->
The Sobol’ total index analysis reveals GDP elasticity and transition probabilities account for 68% of output variance, with developing economies (e.g., Japan) demon- strating a 0.09 elasticity coefficient advantage over developed nations. Dynamic policy layers exhibit sub-15% volatility under abrupt regulatory shifts, while hybrid archi- tectures enhance prediction stability by 23% through systematic variance reduction. Probabilistic calibration is validated by uncertainty quantification achieving 93.2% coverage for 95% confidence intervals, ensuring robust theoretical and operational foundations for model optimization.

<!-- b007439 | heading | pages [24] -->
Evaluation of Strengths and Weaknesses

<!-- b007440 | prose | pages [24] -->
11

<!-- b007441 | prose | pages [24] -->
11.1

<!-- b007442 | heading | pages [24] -->
Strengths

<!-- b007443 | prose | pages [24] -->
Our model offers the following strengths:

<!-- b007444 | prose | pages [24] -->
• 1. Use code for rapid large - scale data cleaning and preparation, filling gaps to enable model prediction.

<!-- b007445 | heading | pages [24] -->
• 2. Seek additional data to validate model predictions, enhancing conclusion reli-

<!-- b007446 | prose | pages [24] -->
ability.

<!-- b007447 | heading | pages [24] -->
• 3. Combine models for more realistic predictions: ,→(1) Innovatively combine

<!-- b007448 | prose | pages [24] -->
Bayesian network and LSTM model for a hybrid structure, achieving model ad- vantage complementarity.

<!-- b007449 | heading | pages [24] -->
,→(2) Employ Cox proportional risk model with Monte Carlo algorithm to pre-

<!-- b007450 | prose | pages [24] -->
dict countries with potential breakthroughs, ensuring dynamic adaptability, ro- bustness, and generalizability.

<!-- b007451 | prose | pages [24] -->

<!-- b007457 | heading | pages [25] -->
,→(3) Apply spatiotemporal regression model to analyze the "great coach" effect,

<!-- b007458 | prose | pages [25] -->
considering spatial interaction and temporal autocorrelation. Use DID principle to control model spatial dependence, making results more robust.

<!-- b007459 | prose | pages [25] -->
11.2

<!-- b007460 | heading | pages [25] -->
Weaknesses and Further Improvements

<!-- b007461 | prose | pages [25] -->
Our model has the following limitations and related improvements:

<!-- b007462 | prose | pages [25] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b007463 | prose | pages [25] -->
• Neglect of the "Great Coach" Effect: In medal - table prediction, we overlooked the "great coach" effect, causing prediction - reality discrepancies. We’ll add coach - related indicators (e.g., experience, past records) to the model for a more comprehensive analysis.

<!-- b007464 | heading | pages [25] -->
• Immaturity of Model Integration: The innovatively integrated model has flaws

<!-- b007465 | prose | pages [25] -->
in hierarchical construction and logical analysis. We’ll optimize its hierarchical structure, strengthen inter - level logical connections, and validate it with more historical and real - world data to improve the logical framework.

<!-- b007466 | prose | pages [25] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b008174 | heading | pages [26] -->
Report on Use of AI

<!-- b008175 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
1. We used ChatGPT 4 for English touch-ups throughout the text, but it was re-

<!-- b008176 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
viewed and revised by the team.

<!-- b008177 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
2. Data Analysis: AI tools (e.g., TensorFlow, PyTorch) were employed to process

<!-- b008178 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
experimental datasets, with parameters and methodologies validated through peer-

<!-- b008179 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
reviewed algorithms.

<!-- b008180 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
3. Visualization: Automated plotting libraries (Matplotlib, Seaborn) generated pre-

<!-- b008181 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
liminary graphs, which were manually refined for clarity and accuracy.

<!-- b008182 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
4. Bias Mitigation: Outputs were cross-checked against human-curated bench-

<!-- b008183 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
marks to ensure ethical alignment and avoid algorithmic bias.

<!-- b008184 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
5. Compliance: All AI use adhered to institutional guidelines for transparency,

<!-- b008185 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
with full documentation of human-AI collaborative workflows.
